(2) The seat's brief told the builder to keep the new row `planned` until Windows proved it; the trace rail fails a planned row that a test traces, so a row flips when its test is written, and the Windows run is the evidence.

## 2026-10-17 — Windows line endings

Spend served the current priority's Windows test fixes, which Jake moved up on 2026-10-17. Git for Windows' default `core.autocrlf=true` and a `.gitattributes` with no line-ending rule gave every Windows checkout of nana-pi, and copier's clone of it, CRLF text files; the TypeScript template's scripts split lines on `\n` only, and seven test files failed on CI's Windows run, two of them by crashing.

**The change.** `.gitattributes` gains `* text=auto eol=lf`. The TypeScript template's code-map, readme-check and trace scripts split on `\r?\n`. The mutation runner creates and resets its scratch worktrees with `core.autocrlf=false`. Rows R-1320 to R-1322: a clone made with autocrlf on checks out LF; both templates' checks pass on a rendered project rewritten to CRLF; a scratch worktree from a repository with autocrlf on holds the committed bytes.

**Plan and build.** The Windows plan (`docs/reviews/windows-fixes-2026-10-17/`: six readers classified the 48 failing files in scope, an agent cut nine lanes, an adversary attacked them) made this its second lane. Claude Sonnet 5 built it in one pass and rehearsed it on the Mac in a clone made with autocrlf on (407 checks, none failing). CI's first Windows run on the lane passed all eight target files; the Windows failing count fell from 69 of 149 files to 62. One reviewer, under the feature-first bar.

**Lessons.** (1) A Windows line-ending bug reproduces on a Mac: clone with `-c core.autocrlf=true`. That rehearsal turned an eleven-minute CI round into a local run. (2) One line of repository configuration fixed more than any test edit could; look for the shared cause before the per-file one.

## 2026-10-17 — the runtime map

Spend served Jake's 2026-10-16 request to build code runtime evaluation into the code map: per test file, the repository code it actually runs, children included, and how long it takes, kept outside the repository and never read by the map check.

**The change.** With `NANA_COVERAGE_DIR` set, the suite runner gives each test file its own V8 coverage folder and writes a record of its path, label and seconds, then a completion record; unset, it behaves as before. `npm run map:measure` (`scripts/runtime-measure.mjs`) runs the locked suite with that knob (the lock runner starts the suite in its own process group), credits each test file with the tracked files its process and its node children ran, and writes the data under the clone's git common directory, so a linked worktree and the main checkout share it. A run that ends without its completion record, or with a count that differs, keeps the earlier data and exits non-zero; a stop ends the suite's whole tree, removes the coverage folder and leaves the caller alone. `npm run map:impact <file>` prints the generator's output unchanged, then the measured tests for each file, longest first, with seconds and any FAIL label, marks the whole section stale when tracked files changed, and prints one note when there is no data. Rows R-1270 to R-1278.

**Plan and build.** The plan had 30 rows, 32 checks and 71 wrong-code pairs after two sol plan rounds. Under Jake's 2026-10-17 feature-first ruling the seat cut it to nine must-meet tests; one adversary added three of them (the real measurement from a linked worktree with no override, a stop sent twice, the zero line's note). Claude Sonnet 5 built it in one pass. The lane's independent check on the runner edit found that a runner check failed under the measurement's own coverage variable, so every real measurement ended red; the seat's real measurement confirmed it, and build 2 fixed the test. The seat's four wrong-code pairs on the runner edit all turned red, and the real measurement on build 2's tip passed 147 of 149 files (2 skipped) in about 13 minutes. In the final round astra said land; sol sent back three points under the new bar: two must-meet tests still passed with their feature removed (the recorded labels and seconds; the impact command's default data path), and an unstaged deletion of a tracked file crashed the impact view. Build 3 fixed all three. The seat then found that the new deletion test deleted and rewrote the checkout's own `.gitignore`; build 4 moved it into a fixture repository. The lane merged main after the rule lane landed, and one confirmation round covers the fixes and the merge.

**Lessons.** (1) A real run of the feature on the real repo found the one bug that blocked it; the fixture tests could not, because the fixture never ran under coverage. Keep the seat's real run in every lane whose feature runs the suite. (2) The cut held, and the new floor earned its keep: nine tests and four pairs covered what 32 checks and 71 pairs were written for, and the one round of send-backs was two must-meet tests that passed with their feature removed — the case the new standard makes blocking. (3) A test that needs a missing tracked file must build its own repository; deleting and restoring the checkout's file is never safe.
## 2026-10-17 — the working rules say feature-first development

Spend served Jake's 2026-10-17 ruling: stop chasing bug-free lanes, build each feature to its must-meet tests, and review for bugs that block the feature.

**The change.** The review standard's MUST is now a must-meet test that is missing or still passes with the feature removed, a bug that stops the feature's main use on a named platform, or one of four harms: data lost or corrupted, a test, build step or seat script writing outside the lane worktree or the temp folder (a product command writing where its contract says, such as install into the home folder, is not this harm), a broken install or hook, or something main does today no longer working. Every other finding is a known bug, fixed when someone hits it. The shared working pattern gives an ordinary lane one final reviewer and no plan review round, and defines the install, hook and gate lanes that keep two reviewers, wrong-code pairs and an independent check. The coding standards and the requirements skill write one row per must-meet test, no wider than the test, and drop clause-by-clause pinning. The seat guide reruns the mutation runner only in install, hook and gate lanes. The builder rules write only the brief's must-meet tests, plus a seal test per new sealed tunable. The pack README's mutation runner line now says exit 0 means the run completed, as sol noted on the split runner. Rows R-1300 and R-1301, with R-1101, R-1108, R-1110, R-1127 and R-1140 reworded.

**Plan and build.** The seat drafted the new text verbatim with six must-meet checks; one adversary attacked it, and the seat took most of its points, most importantly the MUST for a missing or empty must-meet test, the bounded write harm and the lane-class definition. The small-lane builder stopped after five minutes with no commit; Claude Sonnet 5 finished from its edits in one pass. The seat ran the suite and the install check; one reviewer, sol, reviews it under the new bar.

**Lessons.** (1) A rule a builder must follow belongs where every builder reads it: a Claude Code builder never sees pi-worker's preamble, so the must-meet line also sits in the coding standards. (2) A softer bar needs one hard floor: without the MUST for a missing or empty must-meet test, nothing would check the tests the whole process rests on.

## 2026-10-17 — one shared mutation runner, and feature-first development

Spend served Jake's 2026-10-16 report that lanes ran slower than expected. Each lane copied the seat's mutation runner, and the five-way split lived only in the latest copy.

**The change.** `packages/nana-pack/lib/mutation-runner.mjs` is one shared runner; a lane's pair table imports it and passes its pairs and an optional config, which merges an allowed set of values over the defaults. The runner applies each pair's edits in a scratch worktree of the tip, splits the pairs round-robin across five shard processes started 4 s apart, and writes one record. It locks the record file, resolves the tip and hashes the table once and refuses a shard that sees either move, refuses edit and test paths that leave the worktree, and cleans the scratch worktree of a shard that dies. Rows R-1230 to R-1244.

**Plan and build.** The first plan had 40 rows, 49 checks and 98 pairs; the seat cut it to 15 rows before sol's one plan round, whose two MUSTs (two runs sharing a record file; shards seeing different tips) became rows R-1242 and R-1243. Claude Sonnet 5 built it in one pass: 26 checks, all green in 44 s. Ported to the shared runner, the roster-notice lane's 71-pair table gave the same verdict on every pair as its committed record, at one shard (6:17) and at five (2:33); the seat ran it again at five. The suite passed on the build tip (146 of 148 files, 2 skipped, 12 minutes).

**Feature-first development.** While the build ran, Jake ruled that nana moves to feature-first development (the HANDOFF's Next list states it). Under his ruling the seat skipped this lane's own 43-pair run (the table is committed and all 43 pairs apply) and sent the lane to one final reviewer under the new bar. The runtime map's plan of 30 rows, 32 checks and 71 pairs is cut to seven must-meet tests.

**Lessons.** (1) The seat's own checking, not the reviewers, made lanes slow: the regroup lane took seven builds, and its three independent checks found 45, 52 and 47 issues, mostly tests that missed a clause. (2) A plan written row by row grows to match the tool's every edge; writing the must-meet tests first keeps it to the feature.

## 2026-10-17 — the seat works in parallel by default

Spend served the owner's 2026-10-16 request, after the lanes ran slower than expected, that the seat consider parallel work by default. The owner then chose one rule in the seat guide and a new lane limit: two lanes building plus one in planning.

**The change.** The seat guide gains two bullets and a new limit. Before ending its turn, the seat names and starts what waits on nothing else (another lane's build, a long mutation run in parts, separate research agents), while each lane's own steps stay in order. It runs the full suite whole, never splits a long run while a full suite runs on the same machine, and re-runs a check that fails on a time limit alone before calling it a failure. A lane in planning has no build yet. R-1140 is reworded and pinned by four new or renamed checks.

**Plan and build.** A planning workflow drafted it beside two other plans (a shared split runner, a runtime map). sol's one plan round sent it back: splitting runs while other work runs would cause false failures under load, so the rule now says when not to split. The small-lane builder copied the planned guide and test byte for byte in one pass; the plan's pair simulation catches 26 of 26 breaks.

**Lessons.** (1) A speed rule needs its own safety line: parallel work raises load, and load makes time-limited tests fail.

## 2026-10-16 — the builder lineup at every session start

Spend served the current priority and the owner's request of 2026-10-16: show the current builder lineup at session start, which also tells the user nana is active. Asked whether a pi seat should use one builder for everything, the owner chose a lineup the user can change, and named that as the notice's purpose.

**The change.** A machine's roster gains an optional `largerBuilder` slot for larger or riskier lanes, a pi entry or a Claude Code entry limited to the four models a Claude Code seat can start; it defaults to none (the owner's choice). Lane mode gains `--lane-size small|larger`: once the slot is set, a launch with no size is refused, and a pi seat refuses a Claude Code builder before any lock or start, naming both ways out. Both seats show one line at every session start — a Claude Code hook as a start-up message and context, a pi extension as a panel above the input box, never a pop-up that would replace another notice. The line names each builder, the reviewer and the land by model and thinking, never a provider or the unused scout, and ends with where to change the lineup; a relative agent folder reads "roster unknown". `nana-roster --json` prints the lineup to paste back. A new shared rule sends larger or riskier lanes to the larger-lane builder. Rows R-1190 to R-1215.

**Plan and build.** Two critiques and two fact-checks reshaped the first plan: they cut an "open lanes" part nobody asked for, limited Claude entries to the models a seat can start, and moved pi's notice to a panel. sol's plan review sent it back once (a relative agent folder could show a lineup the launcher would not use) and said LAND on the second round. The owner took the three defaults. Claude Sonnet 5 built it in two passes while the regroup lane finished. The seat's acceptance (298 cases) was written and attacked before the build. The lane's one independent check found 42 confirmed findings, most of them tests that did not pin their rows, and one real bug: a home folder ending in a slash printed the full home path. The seat's mutation runner, now split five ways: 70 of 70 breaks caught at 633d41b, in about eight minutes.

**What went wrong.** The seat's own acceptance script tripped two repo-wide hygiene tests (it read the agent folder directly and started a real pi without a private knowledge index); the seat fixed its script. The first builder left a test folder in the owner's home folder.

**Lessons.** (1) A seat script in a review folder is code the suite scans; follow the repo's rules there too. (2) Splitting the mutation runner five ways took a 70-pair run from about 30 minutes to about 8.

**Also.** The owner asked for the seat to work in parallel by default; three lanes are planned for it (the seat rule now building, a shared split runner, the runtime map), with the owner's order and a new limit of two lanes building plus one in planning. The regroup entry below said three amendments and six passes; it was four and seven.

## 2026-10-16 — the regroup check

Spend served the current priority and Jake's 2026-10-14 ruling that turning an existing repo into a feature tree is a core nana feature. The lift could apply a grouping with a proof, but nothing checked an agent's grouping before the owner saw it.

**The change.** `node "$HOME/nana-pi/scripts/requirements-regroup.mjs" --root <dir> --proposal <file> --out <page>` checks a proposal — a lift grouping plus a `regroup` record — and writes a short review page. The lift's own dry run must pass. Each line the lift reports needs one reason, each row one reviewer verdict (keep, move or share, each target with its own reason and one outcome: carried, refuted or asked), each share the proposal adds, drops or moves by a merge a share verdict, each purpose, outcome and reason one accept-or-reject verdict quoting its current text, each gap its evidence, and each row placed by text alone a reason. The page leads with the questions, a feature table and every node with its label and purpose as the lift will write them, then the reported lines, the move and share targets, rejected claims, text-placed rows and refuted items; the full row list is an appendix. A failing run removes an earlier page and touches nothing else. Rows R-1170 to R-1180, R-1184 and R-1187.

**Plan and build.** The plan took three sol rounds and four seat amendments: one for a trace-rail helper the builder had to export, one for shares once the shares lane landed first, one for a share a merge moves, and one for a share riding on a reused feature ID. sol's third-round point (a move refused and replaced by a share) was declined and recorded. Claude Sonnet 5 built it in seven passes. After builds 2 to 4 the seat ran an independent check: five or six checkers attacked in private copies and a separate checker re-ran every finding. Those checks found 45, 52 and 47 confirmed findings, most of them tests that did not pin what their rows claim. After every build the seat ran the full suite and its mutation runner. Build 5 was a closed list with no independent check. sol's first final review then found three gaps: a share riding on a reused feature ID, evidence reached through a link outside the root, and angle brackets hiding proposal text on the page. Build 6 fixed them under a fourth amendment. The seat's mutation runner: 216 of 217 breaks caught at e5d71c6, run in five parallel parts; the one survivor drops a check that build 6's link-aware check now duplicates.

**What went wrong.** The second builder died on a network error after its last commit; the seat ran its closing checks. The seat's own build brief 1 told the builder to read evidence the wrong way, which made every basketball-geek row look text-placed; brief 2 corrected it. Build 3 narrowed the gap test and opened a hole for a status written as a list; build 4 removed a copied status check and opened one for a padded status. Each check round found more unpinned parts of the two long page rows, so after four builds the seat stopped and recorded the rest as residuals.

**Lessons.** (1) A refusal test must read the check's own words from stderr; a row ID in the output pins nothing when the lift echoes it. (2) A row that enumerates a dozen page parts invites a new finding every round; pin each part when it is written, or write the row smaller. (3) Removing a duplicate check needs a test for every input the duplicate caught.

**Also.** The shares entry below said a share line after a table is refused; the lift keeps it. Jake's Windows check passed, but CI's Windows test run still fails 68 of 144 files, so the shares notes' "done is met" was wrong and is corrected in `HANDOFF.md`.

## 2026-10-16 — the lift carries shares; the Windows check passed

Spend served the current priority and Jake's 2026-10-10 ruling, "we should allow multiple features pointing to the same requirements": the lift refused every `Also applies:` line, so no grouping could keep or change a share, and nana-pi's own ledger carries 31.

**The change.** One reader decides what a share line is: the tree parser's first `Also applies:` line of a feature. The lift copies it byte-identical when nothing changes, regenerates it when a grouping merges or demotes features or declares `shares_added` or `shares_dropped`, and refuses every other `Also applies:` line — fenced, in the head or tail, under a kept section, a second in a span, or unterminated (the first share line after a table is kept and moved after `Status:`). The proof recounts each feature's shares from the input and the declarations alone, never from the grouping's result list, so an undeclared drop or add fails. The plan prints one line per added or dropped share. Rows R-1181 to R-1183; R-1167 reworded.

**Plan and build.** A draft and two fact-checks; the proof check's MUST — three readers that disagreed around a fence — became the one-reader rule. sol's single plan round said LAND; its two notes (empty declaration lists mean no change; repeated IDs refused directly) went into the build brief, and the seat reworded R-1181 to one "shall" with the meaning unchanged. Claude Sonnet 5 built it in two passes; the second fixed five tests that accepted another check's refusal. An independent check found no share lost, duplicated, retargeted or invented with exit 0. The seat's mutation runner: 36 of 36 code breaks caught.

**Also.** Jake ran the Windows check on 2026-10-16 and it passed, which meets the current priority's 2026-10-24 "done". He asked whether nana makes agentic work consistent from session to session; a cold-start study (six agents, no memory) found nana's files answer most orientation questions but none of his standing rulings, which live only in memory — so he ruled for a separate rulings file every seat reads at startup.

## 2026-10-15 — the lift: a flat requirements ledger into the tree, with a proof

Spend served the current priority and Jake's 2026-10-14 ruling: "Lift is the tool that enables better regroup, but this is a core feature for nana, understanding existing repo and turn into feature trees, then development really becomes feature oriented". Every existing nana project still had a flat ledger, and a hand sort has no proof.

**The change.** `node "$HOME/nana-pi/scripts/requirements-lift.mjs" --root <dir>` prints the planned tree with each heading's row count, the moves and a proof line, and writes nothing. With no grouping given, it builds the default for a flat ledger: each Part becomes a capability, each `##` a feature and each `###` a subgroup, in file order, labels kept and purposes "Not yet sorted:"; the general engineering sections take the reserved CAP-90, FEAT-900 and SUB-900 to SUB-904. A grouping file (`--mapping`, pinned to the ledger's text) applies any tree, flat input or not; `--emit-mapping` writes the default, or the identity grouping for a ledger that already has a tree, as the regroup's starting point. A separate proof module, which imports nothing from the lift, checks every row byte for byte with its own line ending, a count of every other non-blank line derived from the input alone, blank lines by position where a section keeps its layout, the row-ID multiset, placement (derived from the input alone for the default), the reserved nodes and a clean tree check. `--write` runs only when the proof passes, git is present with a commit, the ledger and grouping match HEAD in every worktree, and the bytes have not changed since the proof. `--verify <rev>` lets a reviewer prove a committed lift against its base, byte for byte. The requirements skill carries the flow for both seats: dry run, the owner's yes, then write. Ledgers with shares (`Also applies:` lines) are refused in every form until the regroup lane adds share support. Rows R-1162 to R-1169.

**Plan and build.** Three research reads (the tree code, six real flat ledgers on this Mac read from copies, the flow) and two fact-checks shaped the plan; they caught an ANSI byte that would have been silently rewritten, a git check that only saw its own worktree, a Windows path break, and a proof that skipped blank lines. sol's plan rounds sent it back twice — the proof did not derive placement from the input, then applied that to supplied groupings too — and said LAND on the third. The seat's checks were written first (110 build cases red on main, 136/136 on a scratch build) and hardened against six ways a wrong build could pass. Claude Sonnet 5 built it in six passes. The second fixed a Windows short-path bug CI found (17 of 28 lift tests red there, all green after). The next three answered the seat's independent checks: the proof first trusted the grouping for its line regions, so a grouping could delete an intro, an owner's section or a field-labelled body line with a clean proof, and `--verify` did not prove. Build 4 replaced region matching with a count of every non-blank input line derived from the input alone, and build 5 stopped a plain 4-column table from losing its header. The last independent check fuzzed about 2,400 lifts and regroups and found nothing lost, duplicated, changed or invented; the four real ledgers come out byte-identical. The final reviews' first round sent it back once more — `--verify` compared decoded text, the worktree scan skipped the grouping file and staged removals, and a tree-form share line could be dropped — and a seventh pass closed all three. The seat's mutation runner: 80 of 80 code breaks caught at the final build, none surviving.

**What went wrong.** The first builder started the full suite in the background and waited for a notice that never came; it sat dead for about nine and a half hours, unpushed, until Jake asked "Probe?". No probe was armed on builder subagents, against his July and August rulings. Since then every builder and every long run gets a failure watcher and a liveness heartbeat, and builders run only their touched test files while the seat runs the suite and CI.

**Lessons.** (1) A builder that waits on its own background job can strand itself; arm a heartbeat at launch and keep long steps with the seat. (2) Compare canonical paths before relating them on Windows: an 8.3 short name and git's long name climb out of the repository. (3) A proof that takes its regions from the thing it checks leaks one case at a time; derive what must survive from the input alone. (4) A test that asserts only "refused" pins nothing when several checks overlap; assert each check's own message.

## 2026-10-14 — every new project starts with the requirements tree

Spend served the current priority: Jake asked why new projects still got a flat list of EARS rows when nana-pi had moved to the capability → feature tree, and a Windows seat on a work project could not run the product report on its flat ledger.

**The change.** Both copier templates now render REQUIREMENTS.md as a tree: a product capability and feature (a DRAFT purpose to replace; the adopt render's feature is `proposed` and empty), then the general engineering rows in one feature with reserved IDs — CAP-90, FEAT-900 and SUB-900 to SUB-904 — so they never collide with a project's own numbering. The rows stay EARS and byte-identical. The seed says how rows are grouped, where a new row goes, that the owner decides tree changes, and how to check. The tree checker takes `--root <dir>` (resolved from where it runs), reads CRLF the same as LF, refuses a UTF-16 or row-less file with a named reason in both the checker and the report, prints one line for a flat ledger instead of one per row, and names a tree heading typed with `-` for `·`. The requirements skill and the five scaffold and adopt skills send rows to the feature they serve, and tell a flat project its path: the lift, then the regroup. Rows R-1150 to R-1161.

**Plan and build.** Three research reads and two fact-checks shaped the plan; the seat cut two report changes as unrequested. sol's plan rounds sent it back twice — the report still passed a UTF-16 file, then the scope still excluded that fix — and said LAND on the third. The seat's checks were written first: 135 build cases red on main, all green on a scratch build, and an independent check added `--root .` and a near-miss heading inside a valid tree. Claude Sonnet 5 built it in two passes; the second, after the seat's mutation runner and an independent check, counted rows by their ID line, as the rail does, outside code fences (a formatter's padded table had read as "no rows"), pinned the reserved subgroups and the no-outline clause, and widened the near-miss scan. The seat's mutation runner: 34 of 34 code breaks caught at the final build, none surviving. On four real flat ledgers on this Mac, copied first, the checker and the report each print one line.

**Lessons.** (1) A count that keys on an exact header line breaks on a formatter's padding; count rows by their ID line. (2) A plan that adds a fix must update its own scope lists in the same edit.

## 2026-10-14 — a seat guide for the session that runs builders and reviewers

Spend served the current priority: Jake saw the Opus seat on his Windows machine check a running worker's work every turn. Builders and reviewers got instructions; the seat got none, and the practice lived only in this Mac's shared memory.

**The change.** A new rule `packages/nana-setup/claude/rules/nana-seat.md` holds eight lines: launch in the background and end the turn (one foreground command in pi); never read or judge a running builder's partial output or re-run its checks; act on its completion notice or a monitor's failure or stall signal; verify once at the end; re-run the mutation runner after any seat fix; send findings back in the next brief; at most two lanes in flight; the builder preamble and the review standard named, not restated. nana-setup installs it beside the other rules (a link on macOS, a copy on Windows); uninstall removes it on macOS and Linux, and on Windows uninstall still refuses to run. The shared working pattern carries one pointer line for pi seats, in third person so builder and reviewer children are not told to act. Passive monitors stay, per Jake's July and August rulings. Rows R-1140 to R-1142; R-301 now names four rules.

**Plan and build.** A planning workflow drafted it; the seat cut six bullets that traced to no ruling of Jake's and kept them as options. sol's one plan round said LAND with two notes the plan keeps. The seat's acceptance and title checks were written first, proven red on main and green on a scratch build; an independent check added the case that the build must be committed. Claude Sonnet 5 (the `sonnet` model the roster calls Sonnet 5.5) built it in two passes — the second made the guide's bullet check count sub-bullets, a gap an independent check found: acceptance 52/52, every named title present, the suite with 0 failures, and CI run 38603676640 green on the blocking jobs. The seat's mutation runner: 19 of 19 code breaks caught at the final build, none surviving, and the CRLF guide stays green as planned.

**Also decided (Jake, 2026-10-14).** Turning an existing repo into a feature tree is a core nana feature: "Lift is the tool that enables better regroup, but this is a core feature for nana, understanding existing repo and turn into feature trees, then development really becomes feature oriented". The queue now runs tree, lift, regroup, then feature-oriented development.

**Lessons.** (1) A seat check that reads the working tree must also check that the build is committed, or untracked files pass every case and never reach main. (2) Fixing a plan after a review needs the scope lists updated too: the tree plan's second round caught a fix the scope still excluded. (3) The `sonnet` builder is Claude Sonnet 5; records that say Sonnet 5.5 mean this model.

## 2026-10-13 — each machine names its models; Windows launchers start pi through Node

Spend served the current priority: Jake's Windows seat could not start a worker or a review (`spawn pi ENOENT`), it treated the worker and reviewer pattern as optional, and no file said which models a machine uses.

**The change.** The user-scope `nana-pack.json` takes a `roster` block with four slots — scout, builder, reviewer and land — and the new `nana-roster` command prints the resolved roster and its source. A lane launch reads its builder from the roster, refuses a bad roster before it starts anything, and prints the builder it chose. A machine may leave the land model unset; then the seat writes the land review into the change, and the slice reviewer's final round covers it. On Windows both launchers start pi through its npm Node entry, run a `.exe` or `.com` pi directly, and refuse every other install with a named reason; lane paths use the native real path there. The shared working pattern gains two bullets — the pattern is the default for any change that touches code with tests, and the seat's land review — copied into `AGENTS.md` and `nana-soul.md`. Nine rows, R-1120 to R-1128; the ledger holds 1,113.

**Plan and build.** A planning workflow drafted it with two fact-checks; sol's first plan round sent it back. Jake's work seat then showed the Windows failure and its own fix through pi's Node entry. Jake ruled the switch "but go through plan review", and we built our own rather than take that branch; sol's third plan round said LAND. Sonnet 5.5 built it in about two hours: the Windows tests first, then the code, then two Windows-leg test bugs the first CI run showed. The seat's mutation runner then found five gaps — a malformed pi package file made the Windows launch throw, the roster check let a rare class of control characters through, a refusal never reached the output file, a stale comment, and a test that could not see the Claude copy of the shared text drift — and Sonnet closed them in a second pass with a named test each. The seat's acceptance passes 122/122, every named title exists, the suite has 0 failures, and CI run 38486971203 at the final build passed the Windows launch tests 13/13. The seat's mutation runner: 62 of 66 code breaks caught at the final build, none survived, and four judged only on the Windows leg. On this Mac, `"land": null` in the roster would drop astra's review and save tokens; the roster default keeps it.

**Lessons.** (1) A pull request with merge conflicts runs no CI: merge main before a tests-first push, or its reds are never seen. (2) Another machine's branch is evidence for a design, not code to take.

## 2026-10-13 — fresh installs take CI's pi cutoff; the install check's leftovers

Spend served the current priority: a fresh install following our own docs got the broken pi-codemode, and the install check's final reviews had left non-blocking points.

**The change.** A new `packages/nana-setup/lib/pi-pin.mjs` reads `PI_VERSION` and `PI_BEFORE` by name from `ci.yml`'s workflow env, the one declaration. It accepts the cutoff only as a valid UTC instant of the form `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SSZ`, and otherwise yields `<version>`/`<cutoff>` placeholders, never an empty `--before=`. The hint install and doctor print when pi is missing is built from it. Every pi install command in the READMEs the readme check covers carries the same values, and a test scans every line of them in any Markdown form. The bench's no-pi fix line names the pinned command. The install check prints the installed hooks and the last knowledge-pull record after any FAIL, finds Git Bash through the path variable in any letter case, and no longer allows two doctor labels that never print. Three install-check rows gained the tests that pin every clause, the setup README no longer says Windows hooks need Git Bash, and Jake's check page labels step 2 PowerShell-only.

**Plan and build.** A planning workflow drafted it; a seat revision cut six unrequested items. sol's one plan round sent it back for one MUST — a cutoff written with a space would still parse and break the unquoted commands — which the seat folded in before the build. luna built it in one 71-minute session with every title named: acceptance 82/82 with a scratch install of the pinned command, re-run by the seat; the seat's mutation runner: 45 of 45 code breaks caught (two only on a test the plan did not name), re-run at the merged tip. After main's process lane merged in, the ledger holds 1,104 rows.

**Lessons.** (1) A single declaration read by name beats a mirrored literal plus a drift test. (2) A seat script that hard-codes a ledger total breaks on every merge; read the base total instead.

## 2026-10-13 — the review standard and the plan rules

Spend served the current priority's process: Jake's 2026-10-11 and 2026-10-13 rulings existed only in notes and memory.

**The change.** `pi-review` reads `packages/nana-pack/prompts/review-standard.md` and appends its text once, after the immutable-checkout sentence, to every review; it refuses before admission when the file is missing, a directory or blank. The standard asks for a last-line `VERDICT: LAND` or `VERDICT: SEND-BACK` and defines a MUST by Jake's materiality bar; LAND-WITH-FIXES is retired and a confirmation round reviews only the delta. The shared working pattern gains the plan rules (threat model per plan, plan-review caps, urgent fixes alone, no edits after an all-LAND review) and the review bar. nana-pi's own `AGENTS.md` carries Jake's 2026-10-12 rule: lanes touching setup or hooks run the install check locally on macOS before landing. The builder preamble carries twelve rules that recur across lanes. Fresh-machine step 8 now trusts the clone before doctor.

**Plan and build.** A planning workflow drafted 17 rows for what Jake called a small lane; the external fact-check's scope list and a seat revision cut it to 12 (over-cap enforcement and a text-placement rule dropped). sol plan r1: LAND 98/100. Sonnet built it in one session (57 minutes with CI): the seat's acceptance 51/51 and title check passed, re-run by the seat; the seat's mutation runner: 53 of 53 code breaks caught after the seat scoped one weak R-1109 check to its own line (50 of 53 at the build). The parallel test runner moved to its own lane: 138 files run one at a time and a locked full run took 725–965 s on 10-13.

**Lessons.** (1) A planning drafter fills a budget nobody set; give it the ruling's words and a row budget. (2) A rule that exists only in a seat's memory does not reach another machine; Jake's Windows seat proved it the same day.

## 2026-10-13 — the install check on macOS and Windows; luna against Sonnet

Spend served the current priority's last appetite item: an install-and-hooks check on both platforms.

**What it does.** `node packages/nana-setup/bin/install-check.mjs` makes a temporary home, runs `nana-setup install` against it with every override variable removed, then the fresh-machine checklist's later steps. It checks that `pi list` registers both packages from the checkout, that a model-free pi session loads nana-pack, and that the Claude Code settings hold exactly the five hook entries in the form for the platform (exec form on Windows). It runs each hook the way Claude Code dispatches it — exec form with no shell, otherwise `/bin/sh -c` or Git Bash — with the documented sample inputs, the three SessionStart hooks at once, and checks each output. Last, it runs doctor. It prints one PASS, FAIL or XFAIL line per item and exits non-zero on any FAIL. An XFAIL needs a sealed known-red entry with the same failure code and a future expiry; both lists are now empty. CI runs it as a blocking job on macOS and Windows before the suite. Run 38406425780: every line PASS on both.

**Build — Jake's measurement lane, built by luna.** Nine sessions. The first three came back partial with the code written and the tests skipped, at well under half luna's context window; splitting work into three-item sessions did not change that. What did: the seat named every required test by exact title in a frozen title check, and DONE meant `0 missing` — sessions 2c, 2d, 2e, 3a and 3b reached it. The seat's mutation runner then did the rest: 44 code breaks, all caught at the end. On the way it found that the test harness never awaited async cases (30 of 81 printed PASS before running), that the check demanded a separate entry per verifier tool while the hooks lane writes one `Bash|PowerShell` entry (so the Windows known-red list could never have emptied), and that the spawn adapter reported a timed-out hook as "no output". One seat error cost a session: a title demanding "the same environment on every child" contradicted the rows, and luna dropped the pi session's `NANA_TEST_TEMP_ROOTS` to satisfy it.

**Merges and sealing.** After the hooks lane landed, the seat merged main; on Windows every hook line then passed, so the seat sealed the known-red list empty, and sealed the Windows doctor note list from a real run — the four hook-file rows and the floor note from the hooks lane, and four pi-only skill skips doctor marks by design. After the CI pin landed, the install-check job's own pi line took the same cutoff. On this Mac the check passes every line at each merged tip.

**luna against Sonnet (Jake's 2026-10-11 measurement).** Sonnet built the hooks lane in six builds; build 1 alone passed every seat check, and the seat's runner, not the builder, found its one regression. luna needed nine sessions here and finished only when the seat named each test and ran the code breaks itself. Both builders' work still needed the seat's mechanical checks.

**Lessons.** (1) A builder that skips tests finishes them when each required test is named and checked by script. (2) A title the seat writes wrong gets built wrong; check titles against the rows. (3) When two lanes share a contract value, test the consumer against the producer's actual output as soon as the producer lands — the verifier-entry shape would otherwise have failed only on Windows CI.

## 2026-10-13 — CI pins pi's dependency tree; Jake's review materiality ruling

Spend served the current priority: CI is how both platforms prove the install and hooks work, and from 2026-10-12 23:00Z every CI suite leg was red for an upstream reason.

**The break.** `@earendil-works/pi-coding-agent` 1.0.2 accepts any 1.x of its six sibling packages. `pi-codemode` 1.2.0, published 2026-10-12T23:00:01.855Z, removed two exports that pi 1.0.2's codemode tool imports. A fresh `npm i -g` of pi 1.0.2 therefore resolved the new codemode. `pi --version` and an RPC session still worked, because the bundled CLI inlines codemode; what broke was any code that imports pi's module graph. On CI's blocking macOS leg 18 test files failed (13 desk, 5 pack), on main too; Ubuntu went from 1 to 19 failing files and Windows from 67 to 69. This Mac kept its 10-04 install and was unaffected.

**The fix.** CI's two pi install lines now end with npm's `--before`, set to `PI_BEFORE: "2026-10-04T06:57:57Z"`, declared once beside `PI_VERSION` with its provenance: the seat's own pi 1.0.2 install moment, which reproduces this Mac's whole `npm ls -g --all` tree. Rows R-1090 to R-1092 pin it: every install line carries the flag (versioned or not), the value is declared once and sealed, and nothing sets npm's `before` through the environment or a root `.npmrc`, which would freeze `npm test` and pi's own installs too.

**Plan and build.** A planning workflow ran three researchers (npm's `--before` semantics, failure attribution per leg, the repo surface), a drafter and two adversarial fact-checkers; their corrections (the install log's package count cannot prove the pin; the cutoff window; an unversioned install line escaping the check) went in before sol's one plan round (92/100, four small fixes). The seat's acceptance script installs CI's own line into a scratch prefix, checks all eight `@earendil-works` packages at 1.0.2, imports pi, and runs the 18 drift files against it, with an unpinned control that must reproduce the break: 23 of 33 failed at launch, 33 of 33 on a prototype. luna built it in one 31-minute session; the seat re-ran the acceptance (33/33) and the ten planned code breaks (10/10 caught). CI run 38399666350: macOS 0 failing files, Ubuntu 1 (the known symlink check), Windows 67.

**Jake's review ruling.** Asked whether reviews could stop chasing edge cases, Jake got the numbers from the Claude Code hooks lane: five final rounds and 16 blocking findings, of which the seat judged 4 material, all in round 1; rounds 2 to 5 (about three and a half hours) chased contrived PowerShell inputs, citations and a stale number in the seat's notes. The rule from now: a blocking finding names a realistic input and a consequence that matters; everything else is a residual; no blocking finding means LAND (the land helper only accepts LAND, so "land with fixes" always cost a round); a confirmation round reviews only the delta.

**Lessons.** (1) Pin what CI installs to what the developer machine runs; a caret range on a fresh global install is a moving target. (2) Prove a pin by the resolved versions and the tests it un-breaks, not by a package count. (3) A planning workflow with adversarial fact-checks caught five wrong claims before the one plan round.

## 2026-10-12 — the Claude Code seat's hooks on Windows

Spend served the current priority: Jake's Windows machine runs Claude Code as a seat, and on Windows nana wired only the knowledge pull. The objective, adoption, shared-memory and verifier hooks were missing there.

**The change.** On Windows, install writes all five Claude Code hooks in exec form: `"command": "node"` and `"args"` holding the script's absolute path in the checkout, with no shell to parse the line. Claude Code documents this form from 2.1.139. The verifier's matcher is `Bash|PowerShell`, and the pipe guard now lexes PowerShell quoting for the PowerShell tool (backtick escapes, doubled quotes, here-strings, block comments, `$( … )` inside double quotes). Doctor checks each of the five entries and that its target resolves inside the checkout, runs the Node 22.18 floor check it used to skip, and prints the Claude Code floor as a note. An existing Windows knowledge entry moves to exec form in place, never twinned. macOS and Linux output is byte-identical to main.

**Plan.** Seat planning workflow (research, draft, two fact-checks), then three sol plan rounds: 70, 86, then LAND 100. Round 1 added the PowerShell lexer and split the spawn evidence into start, SessionStart output and verifier output; round 2 required every lexer case to carry a live `git commit`.

**Build — Jake's measurement lane, built by Claude Sonnet 5.5.** Build 1 was one session of 3 h 20 min (about 905k tokens, 466 tool calls). It passed the seat's 59 acceptance cases and the test-title check, and the real Windows CI leg found three checks that did not understand exec form, which it fixed. The seat's code-break runner then turned the plan's 24 breaks into 29 and caught 28. Explaining the weak one found a regression: the new merge step had dropped main's status-message check, so on macOS and Linux a hand-edited knowledge hook with a stale path was rewritten where main leaves it alone. Build 2 restored the check for a different message and pinned the one surviving break. Build 3 made a missing message count as different too. That first needed the seat's own acceptance seed fixed: it planted a stale entry with no status message, which the installer has never written. After build 3: 31 of 31 breaks caught, re-run by the seat; 59 of 59 acceptance cases; no test lost.

**Final reviews, round 1.** sol (6/10) and astra (78/100) both sent it back. The PowerShell lexer could hide a live commit two ways: a `#` inside a word such as `issue#123` or a URL fragment started a comment, and a `)` inside a comment closed a `$( … )` early. The merge still scanned only matcher-less groups, a second kept behaviour the planned breaks missed. Exec-form ownership went by file name, so a hand-written `C:/custom/nana-objective.mjs` would have been repaired to nana's checkout. Several rows claimed more than their tests pinned: a 5 s limit tested with 10 s, "exactly one JSON line" checked on the first line only. Build 4 fixed all of it: a `#` starts a comment only at a token boundary (PowerShell language spec §2.2.3), the subexpression scan shares the top-level lexer, ownership needs the repository suffix, and the Windows test spawns each hook at its own timeout. The seat added five breaks for these (27–31); all are caught.

**Final reviews, round 2.** sol (7/10) and astra (90/100) closed round 1 and sent it back once more. sol found `<#` opening a block comment inside a word (`issue<#123` hid a live commit), the same class as round 1's `#`. Both found evidence gaps: R-1055's new doctor cases uncited, R-1056 without an existing outside-checkout target, R-1060 not proving doctor never runs `claude`, R-937's skipped rows counted rather than named. Build 5 closed all of it with one boundary rule for `#` and `<#`; the seat added break 32 (37 of 37 caught). astra's nested block-comment case is a parse error in PowerShell, whose block comments do not nest (spec §2.2.3; inferred, not run), so nothing executes.

**Final reviews, round 3.** astra LAND-WITH-FIXES (94/100) on two evidence references; sol SEND-BACK (8/10): the boundary predicate held only part of the set its own cited spec names, so a `#` right after a closing quote was missed and a `)` inside that comment closed `$( … )` early. Build 6 made the set the spec's union and fixed the references. The confirmation round ran over the three-round cap with the standard reason.

**Windows CI.** On build 6's code (run 38347800159, Windows job 115133128863) the Windows leg runs the new exec-form test file green (25 checks), including a real spawn of each installed entry at its own 5 s timeout; the pipe guard (84) and the forced-Windows doctor file (86) pass in that same job. Build 5's run (38342945583) had shown 25, 69 and 86 before build 6 added the boundary cases. Builds 4 and 5 each had one Windows-only test-fixture bug (backslash paths the ownership check could not read), caught by that leg. Still red there and outside this lane: three file-mode checks, a test that deletes its own working folder, and the knowledge and adoption files the triage lists. From 2026-10-12 23:00Z the macOS and Linux legs are red for an upstream reason: pi-codemode 1.2.0 breaks fresh installs of pi 1.0.2 for code that imports pi's modules; the local locked suite is this lane's macOS evidence, and a separate urgent lane pins CI's pi install.

**Your machine check (Jake).** Step by step, in PowerShell, with a throwaway folder: `docs/reviews/cc-hooks-windows-2026-10-12/jake-windows-check.md`. It is the only check of Claude Code's own spawn of exec-form hooks, `CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR`'s form, PowerShell routing and hook latency.

**Lessons.** (1) Every "stays" clause in a plan needs its own code break: the 24 planned breaks covered only new behaviour, and the regression hit kept behaviour. (2) A seat fixture that stands for an installer entry must look like one; the seat's seed hid half the regression for a round. (3) Instructions for Jake are a deliverable: astra sent back "pipe a test run before a commit" as unrunnable, so the check is now a page of exact commands. (4) After a builder refactors, re-run the whole mutation table: the seat re-ran only its new breaks and missed that two older anchors had moved. (5) A hand-written lexer for another shell gets a differential test against that shell's own tokenizer, not character-by-character review: three rounds each found one more boundary character.

## 2026-10-11 — the product report as a feature

Spend served the current priority's last named item before the Windows lanes: Jake asked for the hand-made decision page to become a feature anyone can rebuild. Plan: why → plan → Fable consult → seat fact-check → three sol plan rounds; Jake's checkpoint 1 placed it in the tree and approved its share line.

**What it does.** `npm run report` builds one page from the requirements ledger, HANDOFF's open asks and a store of plain-language assessments, at any commit, byte for byte the same. Every number is computed. Each assessment carries a stamp of the rows it covered, checked against the ledger at its commit with `git show`, so a stamp cannot be forged. The page shows each assessment as current, out of date (naming each changed row) or unassessed. `--packets` writes an evidence packet per stale feature, carrying a ready stamp, so any seat and any model can write a candidate the suite then checks. The suite refuses digits in prose, a `solid` rating over an untested own row, and any schema break; staleness is shown, never failed.

**Build.** Eleven luna rounds. The seat's browser check found the round-5 page was a JSON dump: five "shall show" rows had passed on tests that searched the embedded data, and the seat's own acceptance script had the same blind spot. Rounds 6–8 built the real views from the prototype Jake used (assessment first, status bars, links), checked in Chrome. Round 9 renumbered three rows a builder had given IDs already used on main; round 10 closed five suite gaps the seat's mutation run found (21/21 red after). The plan had no way for a model to produce a valid stamp; the seat added a ready stamp to each packet.

**Final reviews.** sol and astra both sent round 1 back (6 out of 10). astra found the Requirements view's feature filter credited a shared row to the first feature that mentioned it, so filtering by its owner could hide it (59 rows in the real ledger). Both found the refresh guide left out the candidate's feature ID, so following it literally produced an invalid entry, and that several page rows were marked implemented on tests that did not pin every clause. Round 11 fixed the filter, the guide (now tested literally), and the evidence; phone-width wrapping became its own untested row. It also refreshed the installation feature's assessment through the guide — the loop's first real use — because the migrated one still recommended deferring Windows tests.

**Lessons.** (1) Look at a page before believing a test that it shows something. (2) Parallel lanes need reserved ID ranges in each brief. (3) A stored assessment can match its stamped ledger and still contradict a later ruling; "current" means only the first.

## 2026-10-11 — the pi seat installs on Windows; Jake's process rulings

Spend served the current priority: the pi seat on Windows is Jake's daily environment, and the first Windows CI run found its install never registered nana with pi.

**The change.** On Windows, install and doctor run the first supported pi on PATH: `pi.com`, `.exe`, `.bat` or `.cmd`, in PATHEXT order. Node cannot start a `.cmd` shim without a shell, so a shim runs through cmd.exe with `/d /v:off /s /c` and each argument in double quotes. A shim whose path or arguments hold a character cmd.exe cannot carry is refused, not run. A pi that is found but cannot start, or fails its version check, is now a problem that fails install, on every platform — except that on macOS and Linux a `pi` whose interpreter is missing still reads as not installed, because the system reports it the same way. Post-edit reports a missing Windows tool as "could not run" by the rule cross-spawn uses (exit 1 and a command nothing resolves), never by cmd.exe's localised text.

**Plan and build.** sol reviewed the plan five times. Version 1 found pi through npm's global folder, which misses pnpm, Volta and Scoop; version 2 looks on PATH, as the terminal does. Later rounds sharpened which characters to refuse, how doctor reports a refusal, and a conservative post-edit rule. Three luna rounds built it. Two Windows CI runs then corrected the seat's own test design: on the hosted runner Git's `date.exe`, `dir.exe` and `echo.exe` sit on PATH, and no cmd.exe internal command's help exits 0, so the probe now checks help text on stdout instead. An old POSIX-only test also crashed the file before the new Windows test could run. The final reviews then asked for stronger evidence and one safer test: the Windows shim test now stops before registration unless its own fixture was installed and selected, and refuses to launch any other pi. On the last Windows run the lane's four test files pass: the command helpers, pi registration (including a real npm-generated shim that receives a folder named with a space and `& ( ) ^ ;` intact), post-edit status, and doctor runtime, whose POSIX-only probes now skip on Windows. The seat's 18 code mutations all turn a cross-platform test red.

**Jake's process rulings.** A workflow audit of six lanes counted 86 extra rounds, of which 15 caught a real bug: 19 chased rare cases outside any real threat, 23 were builder misses, 14 real design bugs, 11 seat mistakes, 7 empty tests found late. Jake accepted all four recommendations: name the threat in every plan (honest variation in, forgery out); cap plan reviews at one round for small lanes and two for gate or format lanes, and edit nothing once every reviewer says LAND; ship urgent fixes alone; time one lane each with Sonnet 5.5 and luna before changing builders. Jake's Windows machine is x64 with Git Bash.

**Correction to the verdict-reader entry below.** It says the seat's commit during astra's first review voided that round. The ledger shows the verdict was recorded valid: the commit touched only the review-records folder, which the reviewed-tree check leaves out.

**Lessons.** (1) Measure the runner before designing a probe around it; two CI runs went to assumptions about cmd.exe nobody had checked. (2) Find the prior art before the first plan: cross-spawn, already installed with pi, held both launch rules.

## 2026-10-11 — the verdict reader: first-line verdicts, so yokai-world lands again

Spend served the current priority: the land rule is one of its appetite items, and a fault in it blocked another product. Jake relayed a yokai-world session: "I merged by hand. The merge helper refused, because the review tool logged no verdict for any round. … That is a toolkit fault to fix." The land rule read the verdict only from a review's final line. yokai-world's reviewers write it first, with a bare score, so nine rounds recorded nothing and every land was refused.

**Plan.** The seat scanned every review output the ledger names (338 of 364 still exist) and yokai-world's reviews (76 with the verdict first only). Of the 338, 265 end with the verdict, 53 start with it, 12 do both, and 8 hold it only in the middle. The rule: read the final line when it carries the label; otherwise read the first line, but only a plain verdict line, and only when no other line mentions a verdict; refuse when a first-line label and the final verdict disagree. sol reviewed the plan four times (4, 5 and 7 out of 10, then LAND at 10). Each round closed a way to mask a rejection: a backtick-quoted first line, a malformed first-line rejection above a clean final LAND, invisible characters or look-alike letters hiding a label, and a check applied in one branch only. Every check that looks for a label now reads one canonical form of the line; every check that accepts a verdict reads the raw line.

**Build.** Four luna rounds and one review round. Round 1 passed the seat's 64 cases and matched the seat's prototype on all 414 corpus files. The seat's code mutations then found suite gaps twice (dropping Unicode normalisation, then the fold after normalisation and the final-line bare score); rounds 2 and 4 added those tests. The first final reviews both sent it back. sol found the first line checked by the looser final-line grammar, so a quoted or bold first-line verdict above a final LAND recorded LAND. astra found a look-alike in the reader's own table (a Greek lunate sigma) that normalisation turns into a letter the table does not fold, so a disguised rejection recorded LAND; the seat's prototype had the same flaw, so agreement with it could not catch it. Round 3 fixed both: the first line is always held to the plain form, and the table folds before and after normalisation, with a test that every entry reaches its target letter. Two seat errors were also caught before the build: a count of 129 yokai-world files that matched no real file set, and a plan claim that one existing case would stay empty. The seat also committed during astra's first review, which voided that round's verdict.

**Lessons.** (1) A format other repos write needs a scan of every repo that writes it; the land-rule scan covered three. (2) Pin a cross-repo count to a commit; a live repo moves. (3) Run every existing test case through a prototype before the build, so the brief names exactly which expectations change. (4) A prototype that shares the product's flaw cannot find it; the reviewers' adversarial probes did. (5) Never commit into a branch while a reviewer is reading it.

## 2026-10-11 — Windows in CI: the first hosted run and its triage

Spend served the current priority: Windows in CI and the fixes it finds are its next appetite items. Jake asked why CI did not cover Windows; it never had (the matrix was macOS and Linux). He approved a parallel lane and a draft pull request (#1) so CI could run the new leg before any merge.

**The change.** The suite matrix adds `windows-latest`, non-blocking like Linux, with bash as the job shell; macOS stays the blocking leg the release needs (R-1031, R-1032). The support line, one exact string in the three front doors, now says Linux and Windows run in hosted CI with non-blocking legs (R-684). sol's plan review insisted the leg reach `npm test`, not merely finish; it did on the first run.

**What Windows found.** The first run: 63 of 133 test files pass. A measurement run with LF checkout passed 68, so line endings explain only a few. A read-only triage (eight classifiers and a synthesis) sorted the 64 failing files into five product bugs a Windows user hits today — the pi seat's install never registers nana with pi (a bare spawn cannot launch npm's `pi.cmd`), scaffolded TypeScript projects break under default CRLF checkouts, `nana-setup project` fails to refresh AGENTS.md (suspected cause: replacing a file it still holds open), the knowledge hook's stale-path migration is dead, and post-edit misreports a missing tool — plus the declared gap that the Claude Code seat gets only the knowledge hook on Windows. Most other failures are test portability (`.pathname` imports, HOME without USERPROFILE, NTFS-illegal names), and they hide about seventy checks that never ran. The desk's seventeen failing files are recorded only, under its freeze. Record: `docs/reviews/windows-ci-2026-10-11/windows-triage.md`.

**Lessons.** (1) A hosted run on a draft pull request is the cheap way to see a platform before merging. (2) Measure a suspected cause before acting on it: CRLF looked like the main problem and explained five files.

## 2026-10-10 — the land rule: nothing reaches main unless every reviewer accepted it

Spend served the current priority: the land rule is its third appetite item. Jake ruled: "anything landed on main should be accepted by an independent reviewer." Until now `nana-land` landed commits no review covered (it only printed them), took a free-text `--exempt` reason instead of reviews, and read only the FIRST verdict recorded on a revision. A seat fact-check of the live ledger found 17 revisions with two verdicts; in 8 the first was LAND and a later one was not, and 6 would have landed. One was this day's four-digit ID lane, held by hand.

**Plan.** Fable's consult replaced "a review's record rides the next change" with "nothing is written after a land". A seat workflow (four read-only checkers and a critic) then checked the plan against the code and found 16 errors, among them a scope that excluded the ledger code the fix lives in. sol reviewed the plan five times (4, 5, 6, 7, then LAND-WITH-FIXES at 9 out of 10). Each round closed a real hole: a race between the last reading and the merge, an echoed format line read as LAND, a separate clone with its own records, rejections lost before the change, old code in another worktree, marks inside the verdict word, and in-flight reviews of a snapshot.

**What it does.** The round records keep every completed verdict at tally version 2; old code refuses that tally, so it fails closed. A land needs a verified version-2 LAND for each named revision, and refuses when any verified verdict at that commit — any item, or a snapshot of it — is not LAND, when a review of it is in flight, when a commit is not covered, when the source and main are not one repository, or when an option other than the four it knows is passed. Its last reading runs under the ledger lock through the merge. The verdict is read from the final non-blank line, which must be a verdict line in the strict form (markdown marks only before the label or around its parts) by a pattern taken from every real verdict line in the review corpus. The helper prints no HANDOFF or session text. Jake's checkpoint 1: the every-verdict row also applies to the merge feature, whose purpose now reads "integrate only changes every reviewer accepted".

**Build.** Ten luna rounds. Round 1 got the behaviour right but deleted 36 of the 37 land tests (they used the retired option) and pointed eleven rows at one suite test that ran the seat's own acceptance script. The seat added a title-coverage check and split the rest into short checklist rounds; every test came back on reviewed fixtures, each new row got tests that pin it, and the old rows got their exact evidence back. The seat's own run of 15 mutation pairs then found four review-ledger tests whose bodies never ran: a function was passed to a check that takes true or false, so each printed PASS. Build 7 made them run and made that check refuse a function; all 15 pairs now turn their named test red. astra's land review then found that an annotated tag object named as the reviewed revision landed, and that the README missed four declared limits; build 8 fixed both, and moved the post-merge checks and release status back outside the ledger lock. sol's first review stalled on pi-review's 75-second watchdog while running long tests. Its second found that the new parser looked only at lines that START with the label, so `VERDICT: LAND` followed by `Correction: VERDICT: SEND-BACK` recorded LAND; build 9 takes the last line carrying the label anywhere, then applies the strict pattern. The old code showed the same class live: it took the FIRST verdict line, so sol's own SEND-BACK review, which quoted that example mid-text, was recorded as LAND, and the old helper would have accepted the revision. The seat read both reviews and held it. sol's third review found two more paths (a fenced example after the final verdict, and a non-English prefix such as `拒否:`), so build 10 reads only the final non-blank line, with an explicit set of marks allowed before the label. Records: `docs/reviews/land-rule-2026-10-10/`.

**Lessons.** (1) A read-only fact-check workflow before the first plan review found what three sol rounds would have. (2) A builder blocked by an old test deletes it; guard every lane with a title-coverage check and the suite's check count. (3) luna completes about three items of a long brief per round; give it a checklist of three. (4) A green suite is not proof a test runs: the seat's mutation pairs are what showed four tests checked nothing.

## 2026-10-09/10 — the requirements tree and four-digit IDs (one merge); Jake's rulings and the new priority

Spend served the current priority: the tree and four-digit IDs are its first two appetite items. The loop was Jake's new feature process: why, plan, Fable consult, sol plan review, `pi-worker --lane` build, seat verification, sol build review, astra land review, Jake's checkpoint, land.

**The tree.** Jake asked for the requirements as a capability → feature → subgroup tree he can read; the seat first argued against it from an overlap metric and was corrected (shared feedback memory). Increment A re-rendered the ledger into the tree with every original row byte-identical, proved line by line against `6b9202a`, and added R-287–R-294 (the tree checker and `npm run req:tree`). Increment B ran one placement review over all 1,009 rows (17 moved), let a row apply to more than one feature (113 shares over 95 rows in 30 features, flagged as high blast radius), and added feature lifecycle statuses. Reviews found a compressor that dropped the second ID of two-ID runs (74 IDs), G IDs rendered as R, heading forms the checker missed, a field check that called the renderer it checked, and a placeholder bypass that a builder report claimed fixed until the seat re-ran it. Records: `docs/reviews/req-tree-2026-10-09/`.

**Four-digit IDs.** R-1000 accepts three ASCII digits or four not starting with zero in rows and `req:` markers; R-1001 makes a reserved-prefix line that is not a valid row fail, naming file and line. Three sol plan rounds fixed Unicode digits, missed consumers and undefined fences. Six build rounds then made the two rails, and the tree checker, read the same file the same way: CRLF and trailing whitespace (sol and astra r1); one line definition (the seat: the TypeScript rail silently dropped a row holding U+2028); one explicit whitespace list plus one byte-order-mark rule (sol r2); a byte-order mark removed exactly once per file, and the tree checker on the rail's whitespace (sol and astra r3); and the tree counting a row only through the rail's own row test (sol r4). The seat chose the union of both languages' whitespace over sol's ASCII-only fix, which would have hidden rows both rails read today. Records: `docs/reviews/req-ids-2026-10-10/`; the final review verdicts live in the review ledger.

**Seat decisions at checkpoint 2.** The seven desk features stay frozen, now citing Jake's 2026-10-10 ruling. R-224 keeps its share with the display feature: that feature owns the single renderer R-224 requires, so a renderer change must keep the knowledge pull's pointer fields escaped. R-1000 and R-1001 also apply to project setup, whose templates ship both rails.

**Jake's rulings (2026-10-10), from the product decision report** (a published page built from the ledger): nothing reaches main unless an independent reviewer accepted it, so this merge carries its own notes and nothing is written after it; rm may run unasked only on plain targets strictly inside the working directory; Jake requires Windows testing now, CI first, a UTM Windows 11 VM next; the desk stays frozen; a pi extension's default export counts as its named entry point; a study stops when it cannot write its evidence. The new priority line in the umbrella `OBJECTIVE.md` landed after a sol LAND. Jake asked for the product report to become a feature (plan drafted, after Fable) and lifted the HTML-page hold.

**Lessons.** (1) A line-oriented parser shared by two languages needs one written definition of a line, of whitespace and of a byte-order mark, and the same cases run through both. (2) The review log keeps the first verdict per revision only, so two reviewers on one revision can mask a rejection. It happened in round 4: astra's LAND was recorded first, sol's SEND-BACK only in the audit log, and the land helper would have merged; the seat held the merge. The land-rule lane fixes it. (3) A brief that demands verbatim output and a short report gets a summary; have the builder write the output to a committed file.

## 2026-10-09 — Jake's knowledge ruling: builders lose the automatic pull

Spend served the current priority. Jake took the seat's recommendation on the tranche-6 YOUR CALL: every pi-worker child gets no automatic knowledge pull; the seat and typed pi sessions keep it until the 2026-10-23 re-measure; `nana-knowledge query` stays.

A five-agent scoping workflow (three read-only sweeps, a spec, a skeptic) found that only lane-mode `pi-worker` marked its child: non-lane builders (the aml-desk ones, named `pi-worker-a*`) carried no role and would have kept pulling. So the lane is two rows — R-285, the hook's worker skip (twin of R-973), and R-286, every pi-worker child gets `NANA_ROLE=worker` (twin of R-974) — with R-231's log rule amended. The runtime matrix cell for reviewer and worker children now says the pull is disabled by the role (a template change; CI cuts the tag). The skeptic also set the 10-23 method: sort pi sessions by launcher name, not first prompt, and start the window at this land. A `pi-worker` launcher already running at the land keeps its old environment, retries included, so its children may still pull until it finishes; none was running when the seat checked before the land, and the 10-23 reading drops every session named for pi-worker or pi-review whatever its role.

Residuals: `review-ledger run` sets no reviewer role; builder prompts no longer trigger the hourly index rebuild, so the search command reads an index only seat and typed prompts refresh; the pack README's NANA_HANDOFF line names pi-review only. The seat killed two leaked review-test fixtures (eight hours old): the fake pi waits with no deadline for a file whose temp folder is gone.

## 2026-10-09 — hardening tranche 6: instruments before more machinery (five lanes; the program ends)

Spend served the current priority. Same loop: `pi-worker --lane`, sol slice reviews, astra land reviews where the blast radius warranted, `nana-land`. A scoping workflow drafted all seven plan items and Fable challenged them: 6.4, 6.6 and 6.7 became seat acts, 6.2 split into two lanes, 6.3's AGENTS sentence was cut, and the 23 remaining three-digit IDs were allocated (7 used, R-241 reserved). Jake asked how many steps were left after three days of building; the seat offered to trim to the two decision-serving lanes, and Jake said "Keep going".

**Wave 0 (seat).** R-383 regained its closing pipe (the TypeScript rail had skipped the row); the 6.6 compaction-summary baseline was committed (10 compactions, nothing written or picked up since the role marker); four leaked test fixtures were killed (two alive almost eleven days). Sol's review blocked three seat overclaims in that commit — a Part G row flipped from a check that pins one claim class, "0 eligible" from a heuristic join, a writing-trial "stop" from a temporal proxy — and all three were withdrawn or relabelled before it landed. The 2026-10-18 writing-trial backstop binds.

**Wave 1.**
- t6-desk-usage `0278aa8` — the desk writes one `{ts, event, surface, action}` line to stdout on exactly four triggers (page open, transcript view, prompt, app page open) and never carries a path, prompt text, session id or cwd. The desk was restarted through the update runbook; the pre-registered 14-day tally runs to 2026-10-23.
- t6-procs `d872adb` — a zero-model contract test drives the installed pi in RPC mode through the desk's resolver (256 ms, no credentials, temp home and session dir); fixture and cwd hygiene. The seat's probe found that an extension awaiting a UI answer inside `session_start` deadlocks pi's RPC loop.
- t6-knowledge `6b652f9` — every eligible pull outcome is logged with a reason, a corrupt index logs as a failure instead of "no hits", monthly archives leave automatic pulls (explicit `query` keeps them), and the seed lists no nested roots. Seat follow-up: the live sources lost their two nested roots; the-hive's docs outside research went from 0 to 164 indexed.
- t6-skips `8400e4c` — an exit-0 test file with no passing check is FAIL; one pi locator (DESK_PI_ROOT, then npm root -g, then PATH) replaces the pi-absent SKIP branches, so a machine without pi fails the suite naming where it looked; the copier knob reaches the child; git auto-gc is off in children; bounded fixtures; the T17 lock refresh.

**Wave 2.** t6-rows `a5a2086` — `npm run req:rows -- <file>` lists candidate requirement rows for a file in two tiers (direct and transitive test callers), always labelled neither complete nor exact, and prints "unknown (not none)" when no marked test imports the file.

**The knowledge measurement (6.1 step 1, seat).** An analyst and an independent skeptic agreed: of 238 non-archive pointers shown since the reviewer skip, none was opened afterward (Wilson 95% upper bound 1.6%, under the pre-registered 5-point bar). 97% came from builder sessions with boilerplate prompts; prompts a person typed got 8 pointers, too few to judge. The skeptic removed the analyst's one false positive (a `git grep` pathspec that excluded the file). The injection decision is Jake's.

**Lessons.** (1) The seat's own measurements need the independent review its builders get: three overclaims in one seat commit. (2) An extension awaiting UI inside `session_start` deadlocks pi's RPC mode; defer it. (3) A fixed list of file names is a source scanner by another name; narrow the row instead.

## 2026-10-08/09 — hardening tranche 5: install, update, recover (five lanes)

- t5-doctor `d687086` — doctor names the tree pi actually loads (`pi package source`), checks that pi runs (`pi executable`) and that the first `pi-review` on PATH is this checkout's; the README carries an ordered fresh-machine checklist whose versions are read from their sources. Every doctor-calling test runs under a scoped stub pi. A line-based source scanner the seat asked for drew astra's block and was subtracted; the class is a declared residual. Live: three new ✓ rows, all good.
- t5-state `2586703` — `nana-setup state` lists 58 stores by class (durable, rebuildable, re-ratified, disposable, secret), owner and kind; `state --paths` feeds a documented tar backup; a restore drill rebuilds a clean home and doctor reads all good after trust. The seat ended the class with default-deny (only named durable files) and refusal of overlapping or patterned roots.

**Wave B.** t5-remove `c8d37a9` — `nana-setup uninstall --dry-run|--yes` removes exactly what install wrote, read from the state manifest: links into this checkout, seeds still byte-equal to their source, the installer's own hook objects under their declared event and matcher, the knowledge index and the desk plist; everything else is named and left. A scratch home installs and uninstalls back to its snapshot. Reviews found four deletion paths (symlinked ancestors, a nested root under a symlinked `.pi`, wrong-event hooks, a symlinked settings file); each now refuses with nothing changed. The `update` command was cut: the runbook is the sequence.

**Wave C.** t5-hooks `d920725` — the objective, adoption and shared-memory Claude Code hooks are Node entries (the shared-memory hook derives the project key through the same module the CLI uses, including non-ASCII paths); the verifier hook's logic no longer runs on import; install rewrites the exact managed bash entries in place, removes the retired links (dangling included), and install and doctor name any leftover retired command. Reviews found a write outside the Claude home through a symlinked project folder, a TTY regression and a launcher that could throw before failing open; all fixed. Live: install created three links, unlinked three, migrated the entries in place; each hook exited 0 when run by hand.

**Seat close-out** `8f956a1` — R-957 scoped to `state --paths`, three test assertions strengthened from land-review notes; the integration review covered the two seat merges.

**Found on the machine.** A full locked suite can wait half an hour behind other lanes; builders now give that call a one-hour timeout. Uninstall's dry run refuses before listing anything while the desk service is loaded.

**Lessons.** (1) For a safety boundary over configurable layouts, support the default and refuse the rest; modelling every alias took five rounds. (2) Don't build a lint to police a low-risk class; fix the instances and declare the class. (3) Archive by default-deny: name what is durable, exclude everything else.

Two `hang.test.mjs` fixtures from the runner's own termination check had been orphaned for 26 hours (killed; tranche 6). The Codex app re-imported the legacy skills again; a same-day second retire reads ✗ because the backup exists.

**Lessons.** (1) A mechanical gate catches what reviewers read past: the type error sat through four review rounds. Run the gate on every template-touching lane before its land review. (2) A ruling names the exact object it protects, and config is pasted from its file — two seat notes cost review findings. (3) The first hosted run tests the tests' assumptions about the machine. (4) An exit code from a child that may not have run is not a pass; call the gate in-process.

**Wave A.**
